The moment she takes her hand off the lead the mule stops dead, ripping up a few mouthfuls of grass and munching slowly, and Kara sighs good-naturedly when she has to lead it back to the road. “Usually we camp one night and arrive the evening of the second day. The mule is getting a little old and slow, though - the last couple trips, we put him up in the wagon and I pulled the cart most of the way. We just had to swap it back if anyone passed us.”

    Lena laughs, raising a brow. “You pulled the cart? Now that, I’d like to see.”

    It feels like a challenge, and Kara can’t help the impulse to show off a little. She often notices Lena watching while she does any small feats of strength around the house, and having Lena’s eyes on her always makes her feel undeniably good.

    In a single smooth movement Kara grabs the wagon and lifts it above her head, mule and all, and she’s rewarded by Lena’s cheeks going rosy.

    “Kara, come on,” Alex yells from far behind them, intruding on the moment. “Stop showing off, the poor mule has been through enough.”

    “It’s fine, he likes it!” Kara calls back.

    “Look at him, he’s suffering!”

    The mule doesn’t seem terribly bothered one way or the other, letting his harness support him and slowly chewing his stolen grass without complaint, but Kara puts the wagon down again sheepishly. The mule starts walking again like he was never interrupted.

    “Remind me not to get on your bad side,” Lena says, her face still flushed. “Though now that I think it, I can’t imagine you having a bad side.”

    “Everyone has a bad side,” Kara admits, her mind lingering on those first years after her arrival on earth. She’s always been grateful for Alex and Eliza, but navigating life as a teenager on a new planet had been unbearable at times. She’d been constantly homesick, grieving her family and yearning for her culture, and more than a few times she’d taken it out on them. She’d learned quickly that anger here on earth, where emotional outbursts often loosen her control on her powers, is the most dangerous emotion she could have. She has to keep it on a tight leash lest someone get hurt.

    Lena looks at her curiously, and Kara flashes her a smile.

    “But what you should really be worried about is Alex’s bad side. I might be strong, but she once broke a man’s nose for proposing to her.”

    “Hey!” Alex shouts indignantly from behind them. “It’s not my fault that he tried to force his way into the house when I said no! He deserved to get the door shut on his nose.”

    Lena agrees with Alex on that point, and the rest of the day passes in companionable conversation. Alex and Eliza join them near the wagon once their market planning is finished, and soon after sundown they find an isolated spot out of sight of the road to set camp. Kara constructs a quick leaning shelter against a gnarled oak tree and after a quick travel-supper of dried fruits and cheeses by the light of the fire they retire for the night.

    Like always, Alex and Eliza fall asleep almost immediately. Kara is usually the last one to drift off, lulled by the deep, even breathing of everyone else, but tonight there’s one set of light snores missing from the group. Lena is fidgety, shifting and turning over every few minutes beside Kara with little huffs of discomfort, and after a half hour or so Kara finally props herself up on an elbow.

    “Lena?”

    There’s a slight gap between them filled by blankets, but even so Kara can feel Lena freeze mid-turn.

    “Sorry,” Lena whispers, her voice carrying over the chirping of nighttime insects and the crackle of the dying fire outside. “Am I keeping you up?”

    “Are you okay?” Kara asks, avoiding answering Lena’s question. It’ll only make Lena feel guilty over something she can’t help.

    “You’re going to think I’m ridiculous,” Lena mutters, wriggling a little until she’s on her back. Kara mirrors her, looking up at the long yellow grass covering the makeshift roof.

    “I won’t,” Kara whispers back. “I promise I won’t. What’s wrong?”

    “I don’t know if I can sleep on the ground.”

    Kara can’t help it. She’s gotten so used to Lena being calm and collected, never complaining or even admitting discomfort – Lena has admitted that it’s largely because she doesn’t want to appear ungrateful – that this begrudging, almost petulant tone is too funny for Kara to resist a tiny chuckle.

    Lena huffs again, turning over so she’s facing Kara. “There’s something sticking into my back.” Kara only laughs harder, and Lena pokes her in the shoulder. “You swore!”

    “I’m sorry, I’m sorry,” Kara says, trying to calm her laughter. “I don’t think you’re ridiculous, I swear, it’s just…” cute, is what Kara wants to say. It’s deeply, unbearably cute. But instead she clears her throat, sitting up a little. “Is it only the ground that’s bothering you? Not being outdoors?”

    “I think so. I’m just used to beds or cots. Why?”

    Kara, struck by an idea, gets carefully to her feet and gathers up her blankets into a bundle. She steps over Alex and Eliza to head to the wagon, and Lena follows a few moments later as Kara is shaking the bundle out.

    “What are you doing?”

    “Something we used to do on Krypton,” Kara explains while she ties one end of the blankets to a lower-hanging branch of the oak tree their shelter is leaning on. Once she’s tied both sides it creates a sling, and Lena is small enough that she should fit snugly. “A hammock. You can sleep in it!”

    “…that doesn’t look safe,” Lena says doubtfully, looking closely at Kara’s knots. They’re sturdy – Kara has done this dozens of times before when she has the urge to sleep under the stars, and she weighs much more than Lena does. But the strangeness of the hammock seems to give Lena pause.

    “It is, I promise,” Kara says, tugging on the knot to show its strength. “I used to do this all the time. I’ll help you get in.”

    “The last time you promised something you ended up laughing at me,” Lena says pointedly. Kara shrugs sheepishly.

    “Sure, but this is about your safety. I’d never risk you getting hurt.”

    Lena softens. She finally relents and lets Kara scoop her up into a carry, clutching at Kara’s neck with a breathy little noise when Kara lifts her up and over the edge of the hammock.

    “Relax,” Kara says softly, supporting Lena until she’s able to settle in. Lena wiggles around a little, getting herself arranged, and she looks surprised when Kara lets her go to rest her whole weight on the fabric.

    “Comfortable, right?” Kara says knowingly. Lena wiggles again, making the hammock swing a little, but the knots hold fast.

    “It’s strangely secure.”

    “I have one at home that’s built better,” Kara says, turning to head back to the shelter. “I’ll make sure to bring it with us next time.”

    Before she can go more than a step, Lena has caught her arm. Kara pauses, looking at Lena quizzically.

    “Could you…” Lena says, clearing her throat a little. She hesitates, but her hand stays on Kara’s wrist. “Could you stay with me? Out here, I mean. I’ve…rather gotten used to not sleeping alone, now.”

    Lena seems embarrassed by her own request, but Kara’s heart glows.

    “Of course,” Kara says gently, staying where she is as Lena’s hand doesn’t leave her arm yet. She’d stand here all night if it meant not giving up Lena’s touch. “I’d love to.”

    Sadly, Lena’s hand drops away a moment later. Kara gathers her own blankets as the rest of the family slumbers and makes herself a hammock beside Lena’s, swinging gently in the light breeze and listening to Lena finally drifting off.

    She wakes up to find Lena’s hand curled around the edge of her hammock, breaching the distance and linking them together while they slept.

     

     

    Megara is the same as it’s always been – busy, smelly, and loud.

    As much as they’ve been coming here for most of her life, Kara has never loved their trips to market. She had lived in a city on Krypton, but her experience of it had been clean and orderly; cities on earth are chaotic, dusty streets full of people selling their wares or shouting to each other. Gaps between buildings are rank with whatever trash their occupants need to be rid of, and the summer heat feels even worse with no open space for the breezes to infiltrate. It’s overstimulating, and it tends to take over Kara’s senses fairly quickly.

    Lena has no such issue. She seems comfortable in the city in a way Kara has never been, slipping through crowds without so much as jostling anyone – she moves like someone who has worked hard at being invisible, and none of the chaos seems to bother her. She actually smiles as they pass the agora, as if the cacophony is as familiar as her favourite song.

    The inn they stay at is small but clean. Eliza books one room with four small beds to share and a spot in the stables for their mule and cart, and it feels very intentional when she puts Kara and Lena’s things on the two beds that sit pushed together in the corner.

    In every trip to Megara previously, things have gone more or less the same. Kara does the grunt work of setting up their market stall while Eliza and Alex unpack the seasons worth of wares – weaving and medicinal herbs for Eliza, metalworks for Alex – and then the next three days are spent helping out with the selling. Kara could usually find times to wander away, perhaps to buy everyone lunch at a food stand or make a new friend, but she almost never left the confines of the market district. Things were too busy, and if her senses were going to be constantly overwhelmed it was easier to be somewhere familiar.

    Kara should have anticipated that this trip would be different. Kara still helps put the stand together and set up the wares, but this time as soon as it’s set up Eliza presses a few coins into her hand and insists that she and Lena go have some fun.

    Kara, having no idea what in the world that’s supposed to mean, simply lets Lena lead her off into unknown streets.

    “I’ll be honest, I’ve never really been anywhere besides the marketplace,” Kara admits, side-stepping a pile of mule dung and ducking under a low-hanging line of drying laundry. “It’s so loud. Why do people have to shout everything?”

    Lena’s brow furrows. “Is it that bad? You’re usually okay in crowds at home. Most of the village was out and socializing at the olive harvest.”

    “I think it’s more about the amount of people,” Kara explains, wincing when a woman with a shrieking, bundled infant in each arm passes them by. “In the village I can hear everyone no matter where they are, but that’s not a very large population. It’s easier to tune out. Here there’s just too many voices talking at once. Hundreds. Thousands, sometimes.”

    “Does it help to focus on me, instead?”

    Kara has always tried not to focus on Lena, intent on preserving her privacy as much as she can. Having permission to do so feels somewhat voyeuristic, but Kara takes the opportunity she’s given – through the unrelenting noise of the city she seeks out Lena’s voice, her soft breathing, and finally the even, reassuring beat of her heart.

    It does help, and once Kara has indicated so Lena takes her arm in a way that she’s sometimes seen upper-class women do with their friends. Between those women, it always looks casual and thoughtless - it doesn’t feel that way now. It feels warm and exciting, the heat of Lena’s skin pressed to her own, and like a moth to flame Kara follows her deeper into Megara.

    The first place they stop, after ten or twenty minutes of walking the streets and taking in the sights, is a bathhouse.

    “So they really do exist,” Kara says, laughing when Lena nudges her shoulder with her own.

    “What, did you think I was lying?”

    “No,” Kara says, squinting up at the wooden sign above the door. It lists the price of entry and specifies that this is a women’s bathhouse, and people seem to be going in and out of it pretty steadily. “It just seemed so improbable.”

    Lena, one eyebrow raised, untangles her arm from Kara’s and gives her a light push towards the door. “If you don’t believe me, go see it with your own eyes.”

    Kara stops dead.

    “What, and hang out with all the naked people?” she says, resisting against Lena’s pushing. Lena rolls her eyes, seeming to realize that physical pushing is not a battle she can win. Instead she strides forward without Kara, pushing the door open with a wink.

    “Come on. You should see one at least once.”

    It turns out to be smaller than Kara was expecting, and to her relief everyone in the entryway is fully clothed. It’s terribly humid inside and dripping with condensation - there are two wooden doors on either side of the room and a stone countertop in the centre, on the front of which are hung more painted wooden signs outlining various treatments available. Kara has enough time to read the signs advertising salt scrub and mineral bath before they’re beset by an intimidating, heavyset older woman with long greying hair piled in a massive knot on top of her head.

    “Step up, step up,” the woman says in a businesslike voice, waving them both forward. “Public baths or spa treatment?”

    “Oh, no – we aren’t, um. We aren’t actually going in,” Kara stammers. “I just wanted to see it?”

    The woman scowls. “I don’t get paid for tourism. Hot or cold plunge?”

    Kara looks desperately at Lena, who covers her mouth demurely with a hand to hide her laughter. It’s entirely unhelpful.

    “Neither?”

    “We can go if you’re feeling too squeamish,” Lena says quietly, and the old woman visibly leans forward to try and overhear them. “These aren’t for everyone.”

    “I’m not squeamish!” Kara protests, her face hot. “I’m just – I’m just –”

    Kara doesn’t even know what she’s trying to express. She’s not sure why, but she just knows that actually seeing Lena like that – the way Kara had imagined her so many months ago when Lena first brought up the concept of public baths, hot and naked and dripping with water – will bring something out in her that she’s not ready for. Lena thankfully seems to only have been teasing and looks perfectly happy to leave, moving back towards the door, but the proprietor approaches rapidly from behind the counter before they can make an escape.

    “You’ve never bathed before?” the older woman asks, moving closer with her arms crossed. Her face is lined with a permanent frown, and she’s significantly shorter than Kara but even so Kara takes a step back, almost stepping right into Lena.

    “I’ve bathed, just not…at a bath,” Kara says, edging towards the exit. The woman shakes her head so hard that it almost dislodges her hairstyle.

    “No, no, that won’t do. You can’t just go in if you’ve never had a bath before,” she says, grabbing Kara’s arm and pulling her towards the door on the right. “Toxins, dead skin all over the place - salt scrubs are good for first-timers, come on. On the house.”

    “I wasn’t going to -”

    “Follow me!”

    Kara is too shocked to resist. By the time she thinks to simply dig her heels in the woman has already shut the door behind them and started scrubbing her arms with handfuls of coarse salt with a wet-sand-like consistency, and Kara spends the next twenty minutes trying to explain why her dead skin isn’t sloughing off like it should. She can just barely hear Lena giggling in the next room as she gets rinsed off with scalding water, and the very moment she’s dressed again she practically carries Lena out of the building in her hurry to leave.

    “I can’t believe you talked me into that,” Kara says as they merge with the crowds again, running her fingers through her damp hair. Lena holds up a single finger in protest.

    “I did nothing! All it took was a few words from…what was the name of that charming woman?”

    “Desdemona,” Kara grumbles.

    “Desdemona,” Lena repeats, delighted. “She seemed to take a real liking to you.”

    Kara sticks her tongue out, which only seems to entertain Lena more. “How did you do that all the time in Athens? You’d have no skin left. I’m surprised she didn’t manage to peel me like a grape.”

    “I don’t generally go in for treatments,” Lena says casually, stopping for a moment to admire a jeweller’s stand. Kara blinks silently, watching her pick up a bracelet and appreciate the craftsmanship.

    “Then why on earth did you let me do that?” Kara finally says. Lena just laughs and laughs, and Kara can’t begrudge her for it. Not when her laugh is as infectious as it is - like pure joy let out of a bottle.

    The rest of the morning is like a dream. Together they wander the city eating piping hot bread and grilled meats at thermopolia, looking at trinkets, and climbing to the acropolis and look out at the city from the temple of Demeter. All things Kara has never done before in all her trips here for market, being too wary of the noise; being with Lena, none of it seems so bad. Even with all their fun activities, though, Kara notices that as the day wears on Lena seems to get less comfortable. She looks over her shoulder every so often, her eyes on alert at every corner. Wary, almost. Like she’s looking for a face in the crowd, and it’s a face she desperately doesn’t want to see.

    Eliza and Alex do perfectly well without Kara’s help, it turns out, and after a quick supper they all turn in early to prepare for the next day of hard selling.

    Lena falls asleep quickly after the day’s activities, but in the deepest part of the night Kara wakes to find her alert and staring with a strange expression across the small gap between their beds. Lena’s eyes turn away as soon as Kara’s open, but not fast enough.

    “Hey,” Kara rasps, clearing her throat. “Can’t sleep?”

    “Just a little restless. Sorry.” Lena’s voice is quiet, and now that Kara is awake Lena turns to lie on her back and stare up at the ceiling.

    “It’s okay,” Kara says, matching Lena’s low volume. “Do you want to go for a walk or something?”

    “That’s a bit dangerous in the middle of the night.”

    Kara smiles in the dark. “Not with me.”

    Lena smiles back, barely lit by the moonlight through the open window, but it’s restrained. Something is bothering her, and Kara desperately wants to know what it is. “I don’t think we need anyone else knowing your little secret.”

    “What’s keeping you up?” Kara asks, foregoing subtlety. Lena shifts under the blankets.

    “Thinking too much.”

    “About?”

    “It’s hard to explain.” Lena sighs unevenly, her exhale broken by some emotion Kara can’t parse. “I’m so happy.”

    Kara frowns. This isn’t the first time Lena has said something of this nature, and it’s always with an air of mournfulness. Like she’s grieving something that hasn’t even ended yet.

    “You keep saying that like it’s a bad thing.”

    Lena is quiet for long enough that if her heart wasn’t beating too fast for rest Kara might have thought she’d fallen asleep. When she speaks again, it’s so hushed that Kara might not have heard it without her powers.

    “Every other time in my life I’ve been happy, it’s been taken away from me,” Lena finally explains in a tremulous whisper. “I’m terrified of letting myself feel it. If I lost this, if I lost…I don’t know if I’d ever recover from it.”

    Kara wants to reach an arm across the divide. She wants to take Lena’s hand, hold it to her chest and offer comfort through touch. But Lena seems closed off, and the last thing Kara wants is to overstep. Instead she asks the question that burns in her mind, speaking it into the heavy mantle of silence between them.

    “Who are you afraid is going to take it away?”

    Lena doesn’t answer. Silently she rolls over, facing the wall.

    “It’s all right. Go back to sleep.”

    Kara doesn’t. Instead they lie in silence together, neither doing much more than dozing for the rest of the night.

    The possibility of losing Lena sticks to Kara like a burr after that. It’s difficult to understand the cause of Lena’s fear, but she seemed so sure of it. So resigned to her fate. The next night Kara’s dreams are wracked with it – unknown shadowy figures coming between them, pulling Lena’s hand from her grip and whisking her away to a place Kara can’t reach even with all her speed and strength. It stays in her thoughts even during the day when she and Lena are working the market stand with Alex or walking the streets, and it only intensifies the following evening when she and Lena decide to take in a play at the amphitheatre.

    The story is one Kara has heard before, but never seen acted out like this - Orpheus and Eurydice. Orpheus and his wife are happy, content in their love and security in each other, until Eurydice dies an untimely death and Orpheus is so distraught that he descends into the Underworld to get her back. He braves dangers, negotiates with Hades and Persephone, and finally wins his wife’s soul – under the condition that he must lead her out of the Underworld without ever once looking back to reassure himself of her presence. He has to trust that she’s following him, and resist his urge to comfort himself by seeing her face.

    Orpheus gets her as far as the entrance to the Underworld before his will breaks. He looks backward, and in an instant she’s lost forever. No matter how hard he beats at the walls of Hades’ palace, he gets no answer.

    It feels almost too applicable.

    Orpheus’ sad soliloquy is where Kara’s attention starts to wander. She wonders, as the actor sinks to his knees to lament his lost lover, what it means that she relates so deeply to a story that is at it’s core about romantic love between husband and wife. She’s never been in love before; it’s something everyone has always talked about, something people have felt for her but she’s never felt in return, but nobody has ever explained to her what it feels like. On Krypton, matches were chosen by families or through a matrix to ensure compatibility. The line between attraction, friendship, and love is one she doesn’t quite know how to parse.

    Like her eyes are drawn there by some unnatural force, she looks to Lena. Lena is watching Orpheus with eyes made shiny by unshed tears, intent on the performance, her brow furrowed in sympathetic sadness. Her hair is drawn up in a simple braided knot, showing off the sharp line of her jaw and her graceful neck. Her linen peplos is simple, just a natural off-white with hems she embroidered herself with Eliza’s instruction, and she’s just as beautiful as the moment Kara first saw her dressed in silks and jewels. More so, even. It makes Kara ache just to look at her. Nobody has ever evoked so deep a feeling in her – like if Lena wasn’t in her sight, all light would be leeched from the room.

    For a few fanciful moments, Kara allows her mind to conjure a scenario. One where Lena is her lover as Eurydice is to Orpheus, her wife by promise if not by law, and how Kara would feel if she was truly taken away. If Kara lost her to death or whatever unknown forces plague Lena’s thoughts.

    It makes her want to tear the world apart. She, like Orpheus, would descend to the Underworld itself if she had to. After everything she’s lost in her life, nothing would stop her – not even the rules of the universe.

    Possessed by the intensity of her own imagination, Kara does what she’s been too nervous to do until now. She reaches out and interlaces her fingers with Lena’s; and Lena, without looking away from the stage, squeezes them tightly.

     

     

    When Lena leaves the stand to stretch her legs during a lull in customers the next day, Kara pulls Eliza aside.

    “Can I ask you something?”

    “Of course, honey,” Eliza says with a soft smile. They leave Alex to manage the stand alone for a few minutes and Eliza walks with Kara through the rest of the market, a quiet presence while Kara tries to figure out the right way to word her question before Lena comes back.

    After a few false starts, Eliza fills in the blank.

    “Is this about Lena?”

    “Yes,” Kara groans, her head lolling back in frustration. She’s been puzzling over the same thing for so long that her head hurts, and in the end she just says the most direct thing that comes to mind.

    “How did you know you were in love with Jerimiah?”

    Eliza smiles, leading Kara in a leisurely stroll. She seems completely unsurprised at the question. “Oh, I didn’t at first. In fact I had no intention of ever getting married.”

    “So why did you?”

    When they meander past a stand selling fragrant roasted chickpeas from a big stone oven Eliza stops and hands over a coin before Kara can even ask, passing the treat to Kara while she explains. The snack helps Kara focus, and she munches on the handful and follows Eliza further into the agora.

    “You know my parents passed when I was young, and I inherited our property,” Eliza says, and Kara nods, her mouth full. “I was convinced I would live my life there in solitude, working the land like they did. Jerimiah was a traveller – he would come through the village every few months to sell metalworks and he always had something special for me. Never once charged me for it, and never once implied anything more – just a gift and a conversation.”

    “So when did you fall in love?” Kara asks, dusting the leftover spices from her palms. Eliza’s smile gets wistful.

    “We were falling in love the whole time. I just didn’t know it until his gift was a ring.”

    “He proposed out of nowhere?” Kara asks, aghast. The idea of proposing to Lena out of the blue makes her stomach twist into a knot, but Eliza just chuckles.

    “No, no, not a proposal. Simply a ring. This ring.” Eliza raises her hand, showing Kara the piece she still wears. It’s worn silver, twisted beautifully in a serpentine pattern and dulled with time. “He only asked me to wear it, and think of him sometimes as he thought of me.”

    “And…that was all it took? You just knew?” It seems improbable for it to be that simple, but Eliza is endlessly patient with her questions.

    “Love doesn’t have to be a lightning strike, Kara. It’s also built over time. It’s maintained, like a garden. And sometimes our hearts are already in full bloom before we know what’s happening.”

    It certainly feels like that when she thinks of Lena. Like Kara is a spring crocus that’s never seen the sun, opening into full bloom only in the presence of Lena’s light.

    As they approach Alex at the stand again, Kara spots Lena reappearing in the crowd with a small leather bag in her hands. The sight of her lights a spark in Kara’s chest, and Eliza leans close.

    “I think if you’re asking, you likely already know.”

    “What’s that you’ve got?” Alex asks when everyone is in earshot again, nodding her head at the package in Lena’s hands. Lena, looking nervous, opens it to reveal a trove of large, shiny silver coins.

    “Lena!” Kara gasps, leaning forward to get a better look. It’s more silver than she’s ever seen in one place, and she has no idea where Lena got it from. “How -?”

    “I sold my things,” Lena explains, a little breathless from the walk back from whatever vendor she sold to. “My old silk peplos, and my jewelry. I don’t need it anymore.” Lena turns to Alex, whose jaw is agape, and offers the bag to her. “I thought you could use it to upgrade the forge. With a better output, you might be able to get a real contract with one of the city-states. Maybe take on an apprentice. I’m happy to help out in the meantime.”

    Alex takes the bag, staring down at it like she’s convinced it’s going to disappear any second. When it doesn’t she looks up at Lena, her mouth still open in disbelief.

    “Oh, Lena. You didn’t need to do that for us,” Eliza says, but her tone is grateful rather than admonishing. “All your nice things…”

    Lena shrugs like it was nothing, as if she didn’t just sell every remnant of her old life to give them a lavish gift. “I want to help the family. You were so kind to take me in – this is a small token, compared to what you’ve given me.”

    To everyone’s shock, Alex hands the bag to Kara and pulls Lena into a massive hug.

    “Thank you,” Alex says as she lifts Lena slightly off the ground, squeezing her to tightly that Lena lets out a tiny squeak. She drops Lena just as suddenly, her eyes wide. “I have to buy materials while we’re here!”

    Grabbing the bag from Kara again she sprints into the crowd, and Lena stands in her wake looking shell-shocked but pleased. She looks to Kara, her cheeks pink, and flashes the same smile that captivated Kara the first day they met.

    Kara knows then beyond a shadow of a doubt. For all her confusion on the subject, for all her waffling and indecision, there’s no other explanation – every smile from Lena is another burst of flowers in Kara’s heart.

    She’s in love with Lena, and she can’t spend another week not telling her how special she is.

     

     

    Megara doesn’t seem like the place to bring the subject up for the first time, so it isn’t until they’ve made their way home – their wagon more laden with materials and tools than it was on the way there – that Kara makes her plan. The first night after they’ve arrived is too busy with everyone unpacking and helping Alex get the forge in order, but on their second night back Kara musters up every ounce of her courage and offers to take Lena up into the atmosphere in her pod.

    “It’ll be tight with both of us, but we can fit,” Kara says as she pulls the linen cover off, powering the pod up and starting to guide it outside the shed for launch. “I can program it to take us so far up that you’ll be able to see half the earth.”

    “How big is the earth?”

    Lena sounds nervous but undeniably excited, and Kara is reminded how small the worldview of everyone else she’s ever met has been. Even the most well-travelled Greeks hardly know anything besides the patch of land they’re on, let alone any other place in Greece or elsewhere. And fewer still are as curious and willing to learn as Lena is. Lena is different; Lena understands.

    Lena looks up to the stars, her eyes bright and hungry for experience, and more and more Kara loves her.

    “You can’t even imagine it,” Kara says quietly. Lena’s smile is dazzling.

    The pod only has one seat but it can fit two comfortably, so long as Lena sits cradled against Kara’s front. When she settles in Kara’s heart pounds not out of nerves about the trip but because Lena smells like the perfumed oil she bought at the market; her hair is soft against Kara’s face and she’s so warm, so perfectly soft where she presses into the curve of Kara’s body. It’s a feeling Kara couldn’t have imagined before it happened, but now that Lena is here, Kara never wants to let her go.

    “Ready?” Kara says, a little unsteadily. Lena’s right hand is clenched into a fist and her heart is racing, but her nod is enthusiastic.

    With a few swipes of the controls the pod soars into the sky, regulating their inertia with a quiet hum. Lena’s breath catches, but she still leans close to the glass to watch Greece shrinking beneath them. The pod moves more quickly than Kara usually dares to when she’s flying while carrying another person, and the familiar terrain gets smaller and smaller until they can both can truly see the breadth of the continent it sits in – Greece is a tiny patch in an enormous landmass surrounded by unimaginably wide blue oceans, and with every moment new lands reveal themselves. The shape of them is familiar to Kara – she’s flown up here a few times by herself, hoping to track where Kal-El might have landed – but with Lena here she sees it with new eyes.

    “Greece is so small,” Lena says in wonder, her breath fogging the window. The pod slows, but continues its upward trajectory as the spherical shape of the earth reveals itself. “We’re so small. There’s so many places we’ve never seen. Never even heard of. And we think we know so much.”

    “We’re specks in the universe,” Kara murmurs, slowly and carefully wrapping her arms around Lena’s middle. She fears briefly that it might shatter the moment, but Lena leans back into her easily as they look down at the rotation of the earth together. “All of us. All we can do is try to find meaning in it.”

    Lena turns to her, and with their new position Kara realizes exactly how close they’ve moved. They’re sharing breath, Lena’s hands resting atop Kara’s where they’re wrapped around her stomach, and all it would take is a slight bump of turbulence to bring them together. It isn’t the first time they’ve accidentally found themselves so close, but it’s the first time neither of them has moved away. They’re frozen in the moment, the earth slowly moving just beyond them, and Kara only has eyes for Lena.

    “Like what?” Lena says, her breath feather-light on Kara’s face. Kara can see every speck of colour in her eyes, the greens and blues and greys brighter than ever in the soft light of the pod’s glowing controls, and Kara takes the biggest plunge of her life.

    “Like love.”

    Lena’s heart thunders in Kara’s ears but she still doesn’t move away, and Kara blurts out the rest of her thoughts in a rush.

    “You’re so wonderful, Lena,” Kara says, her voice seeming too loud in the quiet of space. She quiets it before continuing. “I’ve been so happy since you came here, and…and I can’t imagine being without you, now. You’ve become so important to me. The most important.” Kara’s courage gives out before the I love you that’s been trying to escape for days now, and Lena’s reply is even quieter.

    “What are you saying?”

    Lena’s face has drifted closer with every word, and Kara gathers the absolute last of her bravery.

    “I’m saying that I’ve fallen in love with you.”

    Lena’s tiny intake of breath feels like it sucks the air out of the pod. Like Kara is breathless too, just waiting for Lena’s next move.

    “Oh,” Lena whispers, and her heartbeat is so rapid that Kara almost worries for her health. She looks lost for words, and Kara fills the space.

    “You don’t have to say anything,” Kara says, giving her hand a gentle squeeze. Her own hands are shaking, and she can only hope Lena doesn’t notice. “Or do anything. I just needed to tell you. Just once, even if you don’t -”

    Moments later Lena’s lips are pressed to hers, and months worth of longing all crash into Kara at once.

    It’s chaste at first, a brief and tremulous thing - just a straightforward press of mouths until Kara realizes exactly what’s happened. Lena looks surprised at her own forwardness when they part, her eyes wide and dark, but as soon as Kara leans back in for another kiss Lena opens to her with an eagerness that melts her fears away. It’s been years since Kara has been kissed at all, let alone with such enthusiasm – it’s Lena who presses deeper, Lena who introduces tongues, lighting Kara up with sensation as she clings to the fabric of Lena’s peplos for any kind of grounding.

    Even with the difficult angle and Kara’s own enthusiasm making her slightly inept and overeager, it’s still without a doubt the single best kiss of her life.

    “Kara,” Lena murmurs against her lips, one hand reaching up and around to cup the back of Kara’s neck. Kara can’t stop kissing her now that she’s started, and Lena’s words are interspersed with them, their mouths hardly parting for air let alone to properly talk. “Oh, I love you too. You stole my heart months ago.”

    Kara sighs, pure relief and pleasure as Lena tugs at her bottom lip with her teeth. It scrapes in a way that makes several new parts of Kara’s body tingle, and even as it’s happening it hardly feels real. It’s too good to be true, too perfect. Lena loves her.

    “I was scared to be too hopeful. To think you could care for me, the way I -” Kara’s breath catches, but Lena’s words of reciprocation make her brave. “The way I love you.”

    “I do,” Lena breathes. She shifts slightly, but there isn’t much room in the pod for her to properly turn around – their kisses remain chaste, hampered by the space. “I do – how could I not?”

    “Countless reasons.”

    Lena raises her free hand to Kara’s chin, tilting Kara’s face with two fingers until Kara meets her eyes. She’s ensuring that Kara is paying attention, making sure she can see the sincerity of her next words.

    “There are even more reasons to love you, Kara. Hundreds of little reasons. Every day you do something that makes me fall for you again.”

    Kara is overcome suddenly with the urge to cry. It’s the last thing she wants to do during such a deliriously happy moment, but Lena’s words are so sweet; the idea that Lena has spent all these months harboring the same feelings she has feels too good to be true. It gathers in a pleasant knot in Kara’s chest, making her eyes water.

    “I had no idea.”

    “I wasn’t brave enough to tell you,” Lena admits, letting go of Kara’s chin to trace those same fingers along Kara’s jawline. “But I should have been. You’re the only person who has ever made me feel safe.”

    It’s a staggeringly intimate, both the statement and the touch. Despite being new and deeply unfamiliar it also feels natural to hold Lena this way, to lean down and capture her lips again. To share long moments together looking down at the world from above with no words needing to be spoken. The stars all around them are as steadfast as they’ve always been while Kara’s life re-shapes itself again within the confines of this pod; only this time, Kara isn’t looking at them alone.

    They land back at home an hour or two later, Kara helping Lena out of the pod and then impulsively leaning forward for a proper face-to-face kiss the moment her feet have touched the ground. She pulls back after a few seconds, meaning to give Lena some space, but Lena clings to her shoulders. She doesn’t let Kara go far and instead deepens the kiss for long, indulgent seconds until Kara is reeling with it.

    “Golly,” Kara murmurs when they finally part, a slight breeze ruffling their hair and making the shed door creak. “Can we do that every day, now?”

    Lena laughs, and pulls Kara back towards the house by the hand. Kara follows, dumbstruck and deliriously happy.

    Alex and Eliza are embroidering by the firelight when they push open the front door. Eliza is clearly trying to give Alex some instruction on the subject, but Alex’s stitches have always been crooked and haphazard, and she looks about ready to give up entirely. Alex looks up at their entry, and immediately her eyes flick downwards to take in both of their appearances. She chuckles quietly, nodding, while Eliza stands up looking utterly delighted.

    “Oh, I’m so glad,” Eliza says with tears in her eyes, pulling them both into a tight hug. “So very glad for you two.”

    “We didn’t even say anything,” Kara says, muffled in Eliza’s shoulder. Eliza squeezes tighter, then lets them go to lean back and look at them both with a proud smile.

    “You didn’t need to, dear. You’re both glowing.”

    “And you’re holding hands,” Alex pitches in, not looking up from her work. Kara looks down, frowning, only to find out that Alex is right – she and Lena are still holding hands, and she’d hardly noticed. Lena is blushing furiously, and Eliza gently takes her by the shoulders.

    “The gods were smiling on us the day Kara brought you here,” Eliza says, patting Lena’s cheek with exactly the same motherly affection she’s always given Kara. She kisses Lena on the cheek, and then leaves them to return to her spot by the fireplace.

    Lena looks like she might cry. Kara squeezes her hand, careful not to press too hard, and Lena leans into her side, pressing a brief kiss to Kara’s shoulder before furtively wiping her eyes and joining Alex and Eliza at the fire.

    For a moment Kara just stands in the doorway, looking at her family with her heart full to bursting. Lena is here to stay, her heart, her love, and Kara has never been happier to be home.

     

     

    Each and every day after their mutual confession, life gets a little bit brighter.

    Mornings feels sunnier when they start with a kiss and a smile from Lena, every sunrise filled with purpose and possibility. Daily chores fly by, and it feels somehow like she has more free time than ever – she suspects that Eliza is sneakily doing more than her share to give them more time together, but Kara hasn’t yet caught her in the act. Alex is also supportive of their new relationship, though she does carve a small wooden sign to hammer onto the forge door to remind them that it’s off-limits for shenanigans after Kara snuck in and distracted Lena with kisses for so long that she almost started a fire.

    In the long, hot afternoons Kara learns that Lena is equally hungry for new sights and experiences as she is herself, and it hardly takes any encouragement for Kara to start flying them on little day trips – together they wander the coast, take in a few nearby islands, and when autumn approaches and the weather starts to cool in the evening they even sneak out to spend an night exploring some hot springs near Corinth. Against all odds Kara is getting a taste of the adventure she’s always craved, and all of it is with Lena at her side to enjoy it.

    Their physical relationship climbs much more slowly, and mostly in stolen moments. The fact that they share sleeping quarters with Eliza and Alex is a major hindrance, which makes Kara work ever harder on finishing the extra room out back, but it’s more than just the lack of privacy.

    Lena is consistently eager for intimacy. She pulls Kara into secluded places whenever they can get a moment alone, whether it’s a few minutes of heated kissing in the kitchen when Eliza is running an errand or straddling Kara’s lap on the beach late at night when no passers-by will interrupt; but it never progresses much further than deep kisses and the occasional wandering hand. Something seems to hold Lena back, makes her slow down and cool off when things get too intense, even if it’s her initiating the kisses to begin with. Even when it seems unbearably difficult for Lena to stop, and they both end up flustered and dazed for the rest of the day.

    As much as Kara thinks about going further almost every waking minute, often waking from intense dreams sweaty and aching for Lena’s touch, she doesn’t ever take the initiative to do so. She has no idea what she’s doing in this department and taking Lena’s lead is a place she’s perfectly comfortable with staying; and besides, Kara is fully aware of how lucky she is to be able to touch Lena at all. Much like how she long ago stopped questioning Lena about her past, she has no intention to push things any further until Lena makes the decision to do so.

    It means that Kara spends a considerable amount of time ducking into the shed to do some deep-breathing just to pull her mind out of inappropriate thoughts, and sometimes all it takes is a single heated look from Lena across the supper table to make her lose all her progress.

    Life continues in this pleasant, carefree pattern for so long that the first disruption to it feels astronomical.

    “What do you think about Anatolia?” Kara says, swinging their joined hands and kicking at pebbles on the path as they walk together. “To visit, that is. I’ve always wanted to see what’s across the sea. I’ve flown above it, but never landed.”

    Lena looks thoughtful. She looks south, towards where the coast lies beyond the hills. “I’d consider it, but we’d need to be careful. Ionia is annexed by Persia, and there’s been unrest recently with the Greek citizens; the satraps might not be welcoming of Greek visitors.”

    Lena says it matter-of-factly, as if it should be common knowledge. But this is the first time Kara has heard of any conflict with Persia, and she frowns.

    “How do you know that?”

    It’s clear that Lena regrets saying anything. Her grip tightens, and when she speaks her voice is a bit strained.

    “I…used to overhear things. My brother had powerful friends.”

    Kara wants to ask. She always wants to ask when Lena drops these vague little hints, but Lena is so clearly uncomfortable that once again she bites her tongue and continues their walk in silence.

    Kara had never really understood the point of walking without a purpose or destination before she met Lena, much preferring flying whenever she got the chance, but Lena so enjoys spending mornings and evenings idly chatting while meandering the countryside hand-in-hand that Kara has become a convert. She now looks forward to their walks more than anything else in her day, and not only because it’s common for Lena to find a sturdy tree to pull Kara behind for a private moment. 

    It appears that it won’t be happening this time, though. Lena looks far from in the mood now, gnawing on her lower lip and not meeting Kara’s eyes, and it’s almost a welcome distraction when Kara hears hard, rhythmic footsteps on the path in front of them.

    Kara pays it little mind besides to move to the side of the path. By the sounds if it it’s only a group of soldiers, heading on their way to garrison or deliver a message. It’s perfectly common, and Lena moves with her easily.

    “What is it?”

    “Soldiers up ahead, see?” Kara says, pointing at the figures now visible in the distance. “Easier to get out of their way now than get yelled at when they get near. They’re always so grumpy.”

    “Soldiers?” Lena says, squinting at the group with a hand shielding her eyes. “From where?”

    Kara looks too, but despite all her abilities on earth, eagle-sight isn’t one of them. All she can tell is that they’re all wearing the same shade of blue, and a quick x-ray tells her their armour is sturdy iron with some kind of sigil on the shields. Hardly anything of note.

    “They’re coming from Athens, it looks like?” Kara guesses, based entirely on their blue textiles and travel direction. “Probably heading to Corinth.”

    Lena goes still.

    “Lena?” Kara asks, feeling Lena’s sudden death-grip on her hand. “What’s wrong?”

    Without a word Lena wrenches Kara’s arm and drags her off the road, fleeing into the brambles with Kara in tow.

    Kara almost resists it out of sheer shock. It would be easy enough to just dig her heels in and bring them both to a stop, but she trusts in Lena intrinsically. It’s out of character for Lena to dart into the underbrush and slide down a slight incline until she can pull them both down to hide behind a large stone property marker, but if Lena feels unsafe, Kara will follow.

    “What’s going on?” Kara pants once they’ve finally come to a stop, ducking down when Lena does. Lena doesn’t explain herself right away despite Kara’s questioning, just ducking her head down with her eyes squeezed shut, so as the soldiers march only a stone’s throw away from their hiding place Kara chances her own look to see what has Lena so spooked. 

    It’s a small group, no more than ten, all wearing well-oiled matching armour over blue tunics with the owl sigil of Athens on their shields just like Kara thought. It is odd, admittedly, to see the matching armour. Armies are usually filled in by militia armed with whatever they can get their hands on, and seeing a standing army force for any city-state - besides perhaps Sparta - is strange. This is an elite group, and highly disciplined judging by their perfect formation. 

    It's then, listening to the frantic pounding of Lena’s heart and the rhythm of armoured feet, that Kara realizes. They look rather like the soldiers that were killed by those pirates in the spring - the guards assigned to Lena by her brother, from what Kara remembers of her short explanation all those months ago. And Lena looks downright terrified of them. 

    “Lena?” Kara says again, quieting her voice. Lena presses her forehead to her knees, her eyes still closed and her brow furrowed, and doesn’t answer.

    “Okay. I haven’t wanted to push you,” Kara says after the soldiers have passed and Lena has shown no indication of moving, “and I know you said your brother has some money, but…I think it would help me understand if you explained it to me. Why are you so afraid? Do you really think a random group of men would recognize you?”

    The only indication that Lena has heard her is her hands, which bunch into tight, anxious fists. Her clean peplos is stained with brown dust, the pretty hem tangled up with burrs; in the distance Kara can hear the crashing of the sea and the now-ominous march of the Athenian soldiers heading towards Corinth, but neither is louder than Lena’s rapid heartbeat.

    “There are things I need to tell you,” Lena says finally, raising her head a little to rest her chin on her knees instead. Her eyes are wet, and she looks resigned. “I should have told you ages ago, but I wanted to keep feeling like none of it was real. Just for a little longer. Life is so sweet here, it’s easy to forget.”

    “Forget what?”

    Lena doesn’t answer directly. She stands up, dusting off her dress and offering a hand to Kara. Kara takes it, careful not to put any of her actual weight on it as she stands as well. “Can you fly us somewhere?”

    Somewhere ends up being a small village near Elis, on the west coast of the Peloponnese. It would be a weeks-long journey by foot, and even flying it takes a while to locate it – Lena seems to know the general area she’s looking for but only manages to identify the exact place by sight, and even as they land and walk through it she looks a little lost. The locals give them strange looks, clearly not used to outsiders, but Lena doesn’t seem to notice.

    “It’s different than I remember,” Lena murmurs to herself, gazing around at the buildings. It’s a slightly larger village than Kara thought from the air, the buildings constructed in an unfamiliar style and connected by alleys paved with stones rather than dirt paths. Not quite a city, but less rural than Kara is used to. Lena wanders them in a daze. Their path eventually takes them deeper into the countryside, outside a short town-wall made of large stones and past a vast orchard of quince trees heavy with golden fruit and buzzing with bees, and Lena only comes to a stop when they reach a half-rotted fence line that delineates the orchard from the plot of land next to it.

    The adjacent plot is about the same size as their property back home. There’s a small house-like structure not far from the fence, dilapidated and long-abandoned with a tangled mass that might have once been a garden and shed, and an overgrown pasture that could have supported a variety of animals. It’s familiar in an eery, empty sort of way. The ghost of what must have once been a happy place.

    Lena stares at the house for long moments, her back to Kara, and Kara tries her best to give her the space she seems to need. It’s only when Lena takes a breath that sounds wet and shaky that Kara comes closer, and she notices that Lena is quietly crying.

    “Lena!” Kara breathes, stepping forward to gently wipe the tear-tracks from her face. Lena lets her, leaning her cheek into Kara’s hand. “What’s wrong?”

    “I grew up here.”

    It takes a second for that statement to sink in, and when it does Kara has about a hundred questions. This is the first voluntary piece of information Lena has offered since they met, but it doesn’t mesh with what Kara already knows. And it doesn’t exactly explain what happened earlier, which she thought was the whole point of coming here.

    “I thought you grew up in Athens?”

    “Only after I was six,” Lena clarifies, turning away from Kara again and wrapping her hands around the low wooden fence. “I lived here with my birth mother before that. Just the two of us. We had goats, I remember. And a big herb garden. It used to smell like hyacinth in the spring.” Lena looks around them at the ripe fruit trees, and reaches out to touch one. “I used to climb these trees to steal the fruits at the top. They belonged to a wealthy family in town, but nobody was ever watching after dark. My mother would boil them with honey.”

    Lena’s voice is heavy with pain and remembrance. The fact that Lena didn’t grow up here for the rest of her childhood indicates that there’s some kind of tragedy in this story, and Kara soaks up the information. She wants to know more – she wants to know everything, but the last thing she wants is for Lena to close up again.

    “What was she like?” Kara asks softly. “Your mother?”

    “She was…she was rather like Eliza,” Lena says with a sad sort of smile. “She used to make medicine for the villagers. But she also did divination. Soothsaying. People came to her all the time for help, and she would consult the gods.” Lena sniffs, wiping the remaining moisture from her own face and finally looking away from the house. “When she died is when I realized what a crock it all was. She was the most devout person I’ve ever known, and it still didn’t save her.”

    Lena is putting on a good show of being unaffected, but not quite good enough. There’s anger there, Kara knows. It’s an anger she recognizes – one she’s felt herself, when thinking about how her parents sent her here alone to carry on the legacy of a dead planet. There had been times as a child, when things were at their hardest and she been overwhelmed and homesick and grieving, that she wished they hadn’t. It makes her chest hurt to imagine Lena as a child having the same thoughts.

    “What happened to her?”

    Lena’s reply is brusque. “She drowned.”

    Kara nods. She could spout platitudes, could give Lena the cliche I’m sorry’s and that must have been hard’s usually reserved for tragedies, but she knows from experience that it won’t help. Lena seems to relax when she realizes Kara is taking the information at face value rather than reacting with pity, and it’s only then that she elaborates.

    “She drowned,” Lena says more clearly, “on the way to Athens. My father’s real wife was found to be barren after bearing him a single son, and he wanted to claim his wartime bastard so that he could have a daughter to give away at the best political opportunity. He summoned my mother to present me in Athens, and on the journey…there was a storm.”

    “But you survived?” Kara says, and Lena nods tightly.

    “The ship made it through. She was thrown overboard.”

    Lena’s hands are clenched so hard around the fence that Kara can hear the old wood cracking. Slowly, carefully, Kara puts a hand over one of Lena’s in a gentle hold, and Lena loosens her grip enough to twist their fingers together in a wordless thank-you. It seems like Lena needs to get this out, and Kara is here to listen.

    “I was taken to my father’s house, and adopted into the family,” Lena finishes, letting go of the fence but keeping her grip on Kara’s hand for a little longer. “But he died too, not long after. I was left with my brother and stepmother.”

    “And it’s them that you’re so afraid of?” Kara asks, gently redirecting to conversation to the original issue. Lena takes a deep steadying breath, letting go of Kara’s fingers so she fold her arms together in front of her stomach in a protective stance, and Kara understands from the tense set of Lena’s jaw that what she’s just heard is only the beginning of the story.

    “I told you about my brother. Lex,” Lena says, leaning heavily against a quince tree. A bee drifts around her face, mistaking her perfumed oil for a flower, but Lena is unbothered by it. She’s entirely focused on speaking. “What he represented in my life. But he’s more than just a man with means, in Athens. He rules it.”

    “He rules it,” Kara repeats, not quite understanding the words even as they leave her own mouth. Lena nods, watching Kara’s face carefully for her reaction.

    “Yes.”

    He rules it. The words are so absurd that they feel meaningless – it’s ridiculous to think that Lena could be royalty, but Lena isn’t one to joke about something so serious.

    “He…you’re saying that your brother is the king of Athens?” Kara says, her throat tight with disbelief.

    “Tyrant,” Lena corrects, her voice and face perfectly neutral in a way that Kara suspects is from a great deal of practice. “It’s an oligarchy. We don’t have royal blood - he wasn’t elected, and isn’t ruling by birth. He seized power after the last king was killed about 10 years ago.”

    “Tyrant,” Kara repeats again. She feels silly just echoing Lena’s words endlessly, but her own words are escaping her. “So he just…decided to rule an entire city-state? And people let him?”

    “Like I said,” Lena says, her eyes cast downwards. “My brother has powerful friends. The Luthor name carries a lot of weight in Athens.”

    “Wow,” Kara mutters, trying to rein in her reaction but mostly failing. She wants to make sure Lena knows that this doesn’t change anything, but it’s just slightly too much information to take in at once. “Wow, okay. So those soldiers really might have recognized you.”

    “There’s a good chance. He liked to trot me out on special occasions so the citizens would be reminded how benevolent he was for taking me in.”

    Still reeling from all the new information, Kara asks her biggest question. The one that’s hovered in the back of her mind since she first saved Lena from those pirates, and Lena avoided her questions all the way to the homestead.

    “Lena, why were you really on that boat?”

    Lena still won’t look Kara in the eyes. Instead she gives her explanation to the dusty ground.

    “Lex had been building an army. Training them, outfitting them in expensive armour. Anyone who asked why simply disappeared,” Lena says evenly, almost sounding guilty as she finally speaks her truth. “Jailed, exiled, or killed. He’d become even more unstable than usual. Paranoid. I was part of a movement to oust him. I thought I could change him, make him see the light, but he…he wouldn’t listen. I had to do something. He was hurting people, it was…it was the only way.”

    Lena sounds like she’s justifying it to herself as much as to Kara, and her lack of eye contact is telling. She’s ashamed – ashamed of doing what it seems like needed to be done.

    “So, when I found you…” Kara says, trailing off when Lena nods.

    “You saved me from more than just a marriage. He’d been planning to use me for political gain since I was a child, but he sprung this marriage on me because he suspected me of being part of the conspiracy, and he needed something to offer to Corinth. A bargaining chip. Something of value.” The last word is tinged with bitterness, and Kara is glad when Lena allows her to come closer and press a kiss to her forehead. Lena unwinds her arms, then, and seizes onto Kara’s hands like a lifeline. “A few hours later, I was being marched to the harbour.”

    “That’s a lot, Lena,” Kara says in a long exhale. “You’ve been carrying all of that alone, all this time?”

    Lena shrugs, her eyes fixed somewhere around Kara’s collarbone. “I’ve always been alone.”

    “Not anymore.”

    When Kara opens her arms in a silent offering, Lena melts into an embrace with no hesitation.

    “I know this is more than you signed up for,” Lena whispers into her shoulder, sighing in relief when Kara presses a kiss to her hair and holds her close. “And I know in my heart that he’s still looking for me. I don’t want to put your family in any danger.”

    Kara rests her cheek gently on the top of Lena’s head. She doesn’t loosen her arms, making sure that Lena knows without words that she isn’t going anywhere. “I signed up for you, with whatever that entails. Tyrant brother or not, I love you, Lena. And I will protect you to my last breath.”

    Lena squeezes Kara with a sudden ferocity, pressing her face hard into Kara’s neck. “Don’t say that. Don’t. I don’t ever want to think of your last breath.”

    “Okay, I’m sorry,” Kara says, smiling a little as she encourages Lena to re-emerge. “Bad wording. But he won’t find you. For all anyone knows, you’re dead – and we’re tucked away from things, at home. How would be even begin to know where to look?”

    Lena doesn’t look comforted. “Don’t underestimate his tenacity.”

    Kara holds her in the quiet for a while longer, letting the ambient noise lull them both into a calmer state. The wind through the trees, the gentle buzz of insects, the chirping and whistling of birds. When they finally part Lena’s mask of neutrality is gone, leaving her looking tired and vulnerable.

    “I’m sorry I kept all this from you,” Lena says, rubbing at her reddened eyes. “It was silly to think I could outrun it forever.”

    “I understand. You wanted to forget,” Kara answers, and when Lena looks doubtful of her sincerity Kara presses two soft kisses to Lena’s eyelids until she finally smiles. “And that isn’t your life anymore. But from now on, no more secrets?”

    “No more secrets.”

    With the heaviness of Lena’s confession eased, Kara finally takes a better look around. The roof of Lena’s childhood home is caved in by decades of neglect, but the foundations are still solid – even the walls are mostly intact, apparently spared the usual fate of being harvested for their good stone by passing vagabonds. The construction of the place overall is impressive, easily as sturdy as Alex’s forge, and as overgrown as the gardens are they’re still lush with herbs and berries. It’s a nice place; the sea is about as close as it is at home, and it’s rare that nobody has touched the place since Lena and her mother left it.

    “Did your mother own this property?” Kara asks, taking Lena’s hand and walking with her through the tall grass. Lena frowns.

    “I think she did.”

    “So,” Kara says, recalling what Eliza has told her about how she came to own their home. “If your mother and father are both gone, doesn’t the law say that the property passes to you? That’s how it works where we are, at least. With no husband or male children, the property has to be passed to someone. Even if it’s an unmarried woman.”

    “I hadn’t really thought about it,” Lena says absently. She looks around, reaching out to pluck at a tangled patch of rosemary. “I suppose it should. It’s such a small place, I don’t think my brother or stepmother ever bothered looking into it.”

    “We can appeal the local council if you’d like. Eliza had to do it when her parents died – she could help.”

    Lena rolls the rosemary between her fingers, cracking the leaves open and letting their sharp fragrance scent the air.

    “I’ve never had anything of my own before,” Lena says, staring down at the broken herbs. The breeze carries them out of her palm, scattering them across the ground. There’s a thoughtful crease between her brows. “Anything that was just mine.”

    There’s something terribly sad about that, and Kara thinks immediately to the addition she’s been building onto their house. It’s close to being finished, just needing a few decorative touches and a bit of waterproofing for the roof, and she’s been waiting for the right moment to reveal to Lena that it isn’t a storage-room after all.

    Perhaps now, when Lena is so melancholy, is the perfect time.

    “Can I show you something?” Kara asks softly, holding her arms out in an invitation to fly. Lena seems happy to leave this place behind, for now – she takes it wordlessly, letting Kara scoop her up and tucking her face securely into the crook of Kara’s neck. She looks confused when Kara simply lands them at home, but she follows Kara around the back of the house to the little door Kara fitted onto its hinges just a few weeks ago.

    “I’ve been working on this for a while. I meant to have it finished sooner, but I wanted it to be perfect. I didn’t want to ruin the surprise so I had to do it at night,” Kara says, not opening it quite yet. She feels the need to explain it first, and Lena raises an eyebrow.

    “Ruin the surprise of the storage-room?”

    “Your room,” Kara says, grinning. She pushes the door open, and with wide eyes Lena steps over the threshold.

    It’s nothing terribly fancy. It’s all the same materials as the rest of the house, albeit with slightly nicer furniture. Kara had been pleased to find that the carpenter in the village she commissioned it all from put in a little extra effort when he learned it was for Lena, being grateful for her more-than-fair prices for scribing, and there are little carved flourishes in the bedposts and the desk. It smells like fresh wood, and Lena sinks onto the wool mattress – it moves underneath her, but the ropes beneath it hold fast – and presses a hand to her chest.

    “This is for me?”

    “You deserve a nicer place to sleep than a cot on our kitchen floor,” Kara says with a self-conscious shrug. Lena lets out a breathy little laugh. Her face is still unnaturally pale with shock, but the reality seems to be sinking in now.

    “This is more than a place to sleep, this is…you built me a whole room,” Lena says, gesturing around as if Kara can’t see the walls around them. “From scratch!”

    Kara doesn’t shrug it off this time. The room feels like an extension of her feelings for Lena, a physical manifestation of the love that’s built up over the time of its construction, and rather than downplay that fact she sits next to Lena on the bed and takes her hand.

    “I’d do anything for you.”

    Lena squeezes Kara’s fingers. Her eyes are wet, tears just starting to form on her lashes. “Even now? Knowing everything?”

    “Even more now,” Kara confirms, and Lena leans forward to press her forehead against Kara’s shoulder. “The more I know about you, the more I love you.”

    Kara can feel the now-escaped tears on her shoulder, and she lets Lena cry them in peace for a minute; soon Lena wipes them, pressing a kiss to the wet skin like a silent apology.

    “I don’t deserve you.”

    “You deserve to be happy, and safe, and comfortable,” Kara counters, not letting Lena’s usual self-criticism pass. Lena considers herself a burden, Kara knows, and knowing the details of her past makes that even more clear; if Kara had her way Lena would never think of herself negatively again. “And maybe someday I could build us something better. I could make you a home again. Something that’s really yours.”

    Lena laughs a little, a cathartic out-breath while she sits up and wipes away the remnants of her tears. “I can’t imagine being happier than I am already. You’ve given me more than I could ever want.”

    Lena pulls Kara down for an experimental cuddle on the new bed – it’s big enough for two which is a new and thrilling experience, and having Lena in her arms like this is yet another reason that the months-long building process was worth it. Lena presses herself comfortably into Kara’s side, one arm draped over her hip and their legs intertwining, and presses a kiss to her jaw.

    “Thank you,” Lena whispers, moving Kara’s hands – which had been hovering anxiously somewhere around Lena’s shoulders, unsure where would appropriate to rest in this unfamiliar position – down to her waist. “For everything.”

    With the decision taken out of her hands Kara relaxes and spreads her hands over Lena’s lower back, murmuring the truth into Lena’s sweet-smelling hair. “I’d do all of it again a hundred times if it made you smile.”

    “I’ve been so afraid that you’ll be taken away from me. That this happiness can’t last.” Lena shifts, pressing herself even closer. Kara holds her as tight as she’s able without causing pain.

    “I swear on my life, Lena. I’ll be with you always.”

     

     

    Lena is so much more free after her confession that Kara could curse herself for not having asked more questions sooner.

    She’s looser with her affection, quicker to laugh and less guarded when they go to new places; with less reason to hide why she’s looking over her shoulder, she seems to be freed from the habit altogether. She stops binding her hair at home, letting it spill in soft dark waves down her back and shoulders, and more often Kara finds her stopping to enjoy the small things – wiggling her toes in the warm sand at the coast, breathing in the fragrant air drifting over the fence during the neighbour’s grape harvest, humming an off-key song Kara doesn’t recognize as she kneads bread in the kitchen. In letting Kara into the truth of her past, the last vestiges of the fear that Kara has always noticed hanging around even Lena’s happiest moments seem to melt away.

    Lena also agrees to visit Anatolia after all, and after Kara has scouted the whole coast from the air she lands them both in a bustling city near the biggest temple she’s has ever seen to while away a pleasant afternoon.

    “I wonder what this city is called,” Kara says, gazing around at the mix of familiar and unfamiliar architecture. It’s far bigger than Megara, and bigger even than what Kara has seen of Corinth from the air – there are more buildings made of painted stone rather than wood than Kara has ever seen in one place, and the streets are wide and clean. Lena’s answer is almost immediate.

    “Ephesus.”

    “Oh,” Kara says, laughing a little. “Of course you know that. Is there a sign somewhere?”

    “No, but you said we’re near a great temple in Anatolia,” Lena says matter-of-factly. “Most likely the Temple of Artemis. The Temple of Artemis is in Ephesus.”

    “I should really stop being surprised by you,” Kara says, grinning. “You’re so smart.”

    Lena smiles complacently. “I used to enjoy looking at maps, and my brother had a particular interest in Persia. It was the only way I felt like I’d ever be able to see the world.” It’s a sad statement, but Lena doesn’t sound bothered by it, now - Kara squeezes her hand, and Lena’s smile gets bigger. Her dimples flash, and it makes Kara’s heart flutter. “Now you’ve shown me more of it than most people could dream of.”

    “You know so much already, though,” Kara counters, shrugging. “I might be able to get us there, but I don’t usually know where there is. Nobody has maps at home.”

    Lena doesn’t let the slight self-deprecation stand. “Only because you didn’t grow up with an archive down the street. And my knowledge only goes so far. You showed me how big the earth is – there’s plenty that wasn’t on my maps.”

    “Maybe someday we can make our own maps,” Kara suggests, swinging their hands gently back and forth as they wander aimlessly. The streets they’re in are residential, but Kara can see them widening just ahead and leading towards what looks like a central square that’s bustling with activity.

    “That’s an idea,” Lena says thoughtfully. “It wouldn’t hurt to write some of this down, somewhere. Maybe give myself a man’s name and get my own writing put in an archive.”

    “And write about flying into the stars in a pod? Do you think anyone would believe you?”

    “They believe Helios does it, don’t they?” Lena replies with a cheeky grin. “It’s not so much of a stretch, when you think about it.”

    Kara laughs, pulling Lena in for a quick kiss, and as she does so they take their first joined step into the square.

    It’s bigger than Kara could have imagined. The agora at Megara is crowded, full to the brim with stalls and surrounded by wooden buildings to the point that weaving through them can be maze-like at times. This square is so massive that the stalls need only line the edges of it, and the centre is free for people to mill about over the bright paving stones. There are benches where people can sit, and merchants selling everything from woven rugs to small caged animals to keep as pets. Kara’s senses are overwhelmed, but she’s grounded by Lena’s hand in hers – and after a few moments to process everything around them, Kara tugs it slightly towards a nearby food stand. Something smells strange and delicious, and she wants to try it.

    Kara could easily spend an entire day sampling the new foods in this square alone. She tries skewered meat and vegetable kebabs, fresh drinks made from fruit juice, stews with unfamiliar and delicious spices, tea brewed strong and sweet and poured in a way that’s almost a ritual in itself; she samples sweets made with honey and rosewater, and a new grain called rice flavoured with bright yellow saffron which reminds Kara of something she used to eat on Krypton. Her hearing is beset with more languages than she’s ever heard in one place, and unique music played on instruments she’s never seen before.

    Even after all that newness to compete with, the Temple of Artemis still moves Kara to her core.

    It’s massive on a scale she didn’t think was possible. She thought she knew it’s size after seeing it from the air, but standing at the base of its colossal columns and looking at the breadth of it from the ground where it was intended to be seen takes her breath away. It seems to absorb the sun, the painted friezes and metopes standing out brilliantly against the stark white, and Kara follows Lena up the long steps to the entrance.

    “It’s so bright,” Kara says, reaching out to touch one of the enormous columns. The stone is soft, almost supple, and she frowns. “What’s it made from, do you think? It can’t all be marble. Nobody could afford this much.”

    “I think it is,” Lena answers, trailing a finger over the masonry. “I can’t even imagine how expensive it was. Most temples I’ve seen in Athens are made from limestone.”

    “The one in Megara is mostly wood. I think the temple at Delphi is marble, but it’s tiny compared to this,” Kara adds, spreading her fingers out. A single ridge in the column is almost the breadth of her hand. 

    “I can see why people travel here to see it,” Lena remarks, and Kara can tell that she’s equally as moved. “It’s a marvel.”

    “I’m always amazed at what humans on this planet have managed to make, even without technology,” Kara says quietly, walking past the threshold of the temple with Lena on her arm. The inside is just as big, a cavernous candlelit cella with a towering statue at the back – it’s made in what Kara assumes is an Eastern style, where rather than folds of realistic clothing Artemis is covered with an overcoat of stylized grape-clusters, her head covered by a crown-like headdress and her hands held out to receive offerings. It doesn’t look like any depiction of Artemis Kara has ever seen, but that only makes it more captivating to look at.

    “We’re a creative bunch,” Lena says, but she doesn’t seem bothered with looking at the statue. She’s looking around the cella as they walk instead, seeming intent on something, and when she finds what she’s looking for she pulls Kara towards it with confidence. She leads Kara around the back of the statue, watching to ensure nobody is paying attention to them, before opening a small, painted wooden door that Kara hadn’t noticed before and pulling Kara through it.

    The room beyond is dark, lit only by some distant light from above where strategic gaps in the walls let in the sunlight, and Kara can dimly see shelves and tables around them covered in shadowy shapes. She brushes up against one of them and something jingles and then clatters to the floor, and Kara feels around for it to find that it’s a smooth metal cup full of coins.

    “Wait,” Kara says, standing up again. “Are we in the adyton? This is the offering-room, Lena, we’re not allowed to be here!”

    “It’s fine, the priest just left,” Lena replies, her voice low and honeyed. She presses gently on Kara’s chest, and Kara follows the direction until she’s backed up against a cool stone wall. “We should have a few minutes.”

    “A few minutes for what?”

    Lena’s first kiss misses Kara’s mouth in the dark and lands instead somewhere near her chin, but Kara still gets the gist. And as much as being in a forbidden room in a strange city is terrifying, her desire for Lena wins out in the end; she cups Lena’s jaw and directs her slightly upwards, slotting their mouths together in a kiss that’s intense from the first moment.

    “It feels strange to do this in a temple,” Kara pants even as Lena is nipping at her bottom lip, pulling Kara’s hands to her body. She bypasses the waist this time and brings them right to her own hips, and in a fit of inspiration Kara spins them around to pin Lena to the wall instead. The trees have mostly grown back, though a few still lie splintered and untouched, while the deep scar in the rock remains from Kara’s crash landing. Lena crouches down to touch it.

    “I can’t imagine how alone you felt,” she says, her brow furrowed. “Crashing here by yourself in the middle of the mountains, not even knowing the language. After all you’d been through.”

    Kara nods. It feels more solemn than she expected, being here again – she’s come back to the site a few times by herself, but with Lena it’s seems more real than it did before. “I don’t know what would have happened if Eliza and Alex hadn’t been there. I could have landed anywhere, but I ended up here. Exactly where they were camped.”

    “You could have landed anywhere,” Lena repeats softly, leaning into Kara. “And I never would have met you.”

    Kara puts an arm around her, kissing the top of her head soundly. “Let’s go give thanks to Apollo for that, huh?”

    Lena chuckles, then, rolling her eyes and letting Kara scoop her up for another short flight. “Right. I’m sure the almighty god of the sun is very concerned with my emotional welfa -”

    The word fades into a laughing-scream as Kara takes off without warning, shooting into the sky with Lena clinging to her neck.

    The sanctuary is bustling with activity when they arrive – the ritual procession happened earlier in the day and Lena had been insistent about not needing to witness another one, so the Games are in full swing by the time they land in the woods and blend into the mass of spectators. They watch the sprints and the pankration with the rest of the crowd, but Lena seems rather off-put by the sheer volume of oiled male bodies in the wrestling matches – once the musical competitions start, though, she comes alive. She claps along with the songs with everyone else, cheers for the musicians she likes the most, and joins in the booing when an inferior winner is chosen.

    “That was absurd,” Lena grumbles when they exit the theatre, following the crowd towards the banquet set up near the temple. “It was clear that the winner was pre-determined – I bet he paid a judge.”

    Kara laughs, putting a consoling arm around Lena’s shoulder. “It’s the experience of watching that matters, though, right?”

    “Right,” Lena says, nodding, but there’s still a furrow between her brows. They walk in silence for a few moments, but soon enough Lena’s irritation bursts out again.

    “But what’s the point of it all supposedly being for Apollo if they aren’t actually honouring the best talent? Isn’t he the god of truth and prophecy? You’d think that if they thought he was real they wouldn’t want to lie -”

    Lena’s rant lasts all the way to the temple, and Kara is happy to listen.

    The tables are overfilled with food and wine, salted fish and meat from the sacrifices and festive barley cakes, and after they’ve both eaten their fill – Kara significantly more than Lena, managing to covertly scoop up four helpings – Kara leads them both to the open grassy area set aside for dancing. It’s delineated by poles strung with colourful fabric banners and already crowded with people moving to the beat of the live musicians, and with each song Lena dances a little more freely.
